FOREWORD 

During recent years all of us have become increasingly aware of the need for attaining more effective and efficient Government administration. In the words of the Hoover Commission:

There is perhaps no time in history when it has been more important to evaluate the effectiveness of the Executive Branch of the Government in carrying out the will of the Congress and the people. While we recognize that efficiency in itself is no guarantee of democratic government, the sobering fact remains that the highest aims and ideals of democracy can be thwarted through excessive administrative costs and through waste, disunity, apathy, irresponsibility, and other byproducts of inefficient government. 

One of the most important prerequisites of effective, efficient administration in an agency of the size, complexity, and geographic spread of the Department of the Interior is a clear understanding by its officials and employees of the structure and division of functions of the Department as a whole and of its constituent bureaus and offices-in other words, of its organization. The Official Organization Handbook of the Department attempts to foster such an understanding. It is a statement of approved organization, subject to such improvements as may be found practicable and necessary from time to time.. 

As such, the handbook should be of much value to personnel of the Department and also to the public. gating 

Secretary of the Interior.

1.00. Creation and functions.-The Departnt of the Interior was created by the act of rch 3, 1849. The work of the Department ls with the management, conservation, and deopment of the natural resources of the United tes. These resources include the public lands the Federal range, water and power resources, gas, and other mineral resources, certain forest ources, fish and wildlife resources, and the cional park system. In addition, the Departn of the Interior is responsible for the administion of Indian Affairs and for certain functions the territories and island possessions of the ited States.

A3.00. The Secretariat of the Department.— 

The chief officers of the Department of the Interior are the Secretary, the Under Secretary, and two Assistant Secretaries.

A3.01. The Secretary.-The Secretary of the 

Interior, as head of the Department and as a Cabinet officer directly responsible to the President of the United States, is charged with the supervision and direction of the activities carried on by all bureaus and offices of the Department in administering the functions within its jurisdiction. 1

2 

Organization Handbook A3.02. The Under Secretary.-The Under Sec retary advises and assists the Secretary in the determination and execution of Departmental policy and programs. He serves as the Secretary's principal adviser and as Acting Secretary of the Interior during the absence or other unavailability of the Secretary. The Under Secretary assists the 

Secretary in directing the Department's liaison relationships with other Federal Departments, the 

Congress and the public.

A3.03. The Assistant Secretaries.-The two 

Assistant Secretaries are coordinate in rank, and together with the Under Secretary they assist the Secretary in the determination and execution of policies and programs, and in dealing with other Government agencies and with the public. In the absence or unavailability of the Secretary and 

Under Secretary, an Assistant Secretary serves as the Acting Secretary of the Interior..

A3.06. The Office of the Secretary.-In gen eral, the Office of the Secretary, in addition to the Immediate Office of the Secretary, is composed of units performing staff functions which relate to responsibilities exercised by the Secretary; which are common to several bureaus and offices of the Department and cannot be performed effectively by any one of such bureaus and officers; and which are inherent in the Secretary's general responsi bility as the chief official of the Department and as chief adviser to the President in the field of natural

ources, Indian matters, and territories and and possessions. Further, the Office of the Secary includes the Office of the Solicitor of the partment.

The units in the Office of the Secretary, exclue of the Office of the Solicitor, are of two types, mely, program staff and administrative staff. e program staff units are concerned with the ostantive programs and policies of the Departnt with respect to consistency, timing emphaand general adequacy. These units review oposed programs, policies and legislation; they amine operating reports; make special program dies; and advise the Secretariat and interested reau heads concerning pertinent aspects of the epartment's programs. In addition, the proam units perform certain limited duties of an erational nature in special fields not assignable any one bureau or other operating agency. 

The administrative staff units on the other hand e concerned with the means to facilitate execuon of approved programs of the Department. hey advise and assist the Secretariat, as well as e heads of operating bureaus and offices, in the eneral field of administrative and personnel anagement. Administrative management funcons are decentralized to bureaus and operating gencies to a large extent by law and by Secretarial elegation. The administrative staff units mainan relationships with administrative manageent agencies of the executive branch of the overnment; formulate standards for consideraion of and action by the Secretary or promulgate uch standards under delegation by the Secretary; ssist bureaus and offices to improve their adminisrative operations; inspect such operations for onformity with laws and Secretarial orders, and erform administrative services for the Office of 

The Secretary.

A3.06c. Relationship of the Office of the Sec retary to bureaus and other agencies.-The 

Department of the Interior bureaus and other agencies are authorized, organized and staffed to perform virtually all operating functions and necessary supporting services. Bureaus and other operating agencies report directly to the Secretariat.

Functions performed by the units of the Office of the Secretary affecting bureau programs and activities are performed for the Secretary or by delegation from him.

Organization Handbook A3.02. The Under Secretary.-The Under Sec retary advises and assists the Secretary in the determination and execution of Departmental policy and programs. He serves as the Secretary's principal adviser and as Acting Secretary of the Interior during the absence or other unavailability of the Secretary. The Under Secretary assists the

(d) Major problem areas. From time to time, the Secretary assigns major problems relating to the development and conservation of natural resources to an Assistant Secretary for special attention. These major problem areas may relate to geographic or functional areas such as Development of Alaska, Development of the Pacific Northwest, Rehabilitation of the Navajo and Hopi Indians, the Stockpiling Program and similar problems. Where special problem areas are assigned, the bureaus and offices report to the appropriate Assistant Secretary on activities related to the assigned major problem area without regard to the assignment made by the Secretary for general, supervisory purposes. 

Each Assistant Secretary is charged with the responsibility of formulating a departmental program and maintaining continuous supervision over the departmental and bureau programs and activities in problem areas assigned to him. 

(e) Delegation of authority.-The Secretary and Under Secretary may refer to an Assistant Secretary matters to which they are unable to give their personal attention. With respect to supervision of bureaus and offices, on most other matters which come before them, the Under Secretary and Assistant Secretaries exercise all of the authority of the Secretary. 

(f) The Solicitor as Acting Secretary.—The 

Solicitor of the Department of the Interior is empowered to serve as Acting Secretary, Acting 

Under Secretary, or Acting Assistant Secretary in certain circumstances.

A3.05. The immediate Office of the Secre tary.—The Secretariat of the Department, having no large organization under their immediate administrative supervision, depend chiefly upon the personnel and facilities of the  for staff assistance in accomplishing their functions. The Secretariat have several special assistants, confidential secretaries and clerks, which, together with the Secretariat constitute the 

Immediate Office of the Secretary.

A3.06. The Office of the Secretary.-In gen eral, the Office of the Secretary, in addition to the Immediate Office of the Secretary, is composed of units performing staff functions which relate to responsibilities exercised by the Secretary; which are common to several bureaus and offices of the Department and cannot be performed effectively by any one of such bureaus and officers; and which are inherent in the Secretary's general responsibility as the chief official of the Department and as chief adviser to the President in the field of natural

5 resources, Indian matters, and territories and island possessions. Further, the Office of the Secretary includes the Office of the Solicitor of the Department.

The units in the Office of the Secretary, exclusive of the Office of the Solicitor, are of two types, namely, program staff and administrative staff. 

The program staff units are concerned with the substantive programs and policies of the Department with respect to consistency, timing emphasis, and general adequacy. These units review proposed programs, policies and legislation; they examine operating reports; make special program studies; and advise the Secretariat and interested bureau heads concerning pertinent aspects of the Department's programs. In addition, the program units perform certain limited duties of an operational nature in special fields not assignable to any one bureau or other operating agency. 

The administrative staff units on the other hand are concerned with the means to facilitate execution of approved programs of the Department. 

They advise and assist the Secretariat, as well as the heads of operating bureaus and offices, in the general field of administrative and personnel management. Administrative management functions are decentralized to bureaus and operating agencies to a large extent by law and by Secretarial delegation. The administrative staff units maintian relationships with administrative management agencies of the executive branch of the Government; formulate standards for consideration of and action by the Secretary or promulgate such standards under delegation by the Secretary; assist bureaus and offices to improve their administrative operations; inspect such operations for conformity with laws and Secretarial orders, and perform administrative services for the Office of the Secretary.

Functions performed by the units of the Office of the Secretary affecting bureau programs and activities are performed for the Secretary or by delegation from him.

A3.06e. Departmental field committees.- 

These committees, which report to the Secretary through the Director of the Program Staff as Chairman of the Program Committee, are designed to improve the facilities for assuring that the Department's programs in the several geographical regions of the United States and its territories, are integrated and internally consistent, appropriately related to the programs of other Federal, state and local agencies in the region, and meet the resource needs of the region and the nation.

Each field committee consists of a chairman who represents the Secretary and a member from each agency of the Department which engages in field. operations in the region.

Field committees have been established in the following regions:

Region 1-Alaska. 

Region 2-Pacific Northwest. 

Region 5-Missouri River Basin. 

Region 6-Southwest. 

Additional field committees will be formed as rapidly as is practical.

A3.07. Office of the Solicitor.-As chief law officer of the Department, the Solicitor is the principal legal adviser of the Secretary and of other departmental officers. He exercises professional supervision and direction over the legal work of the entire Department and over all the legal personnel employed by the Department and its several agencies.

The Office of the Solicitor consists of four divisions, each of which is headed by an Assistant Solicitor, and an administrative branch, which is headed by an Administrative Officer.

A3.07a. The Division of Claims has jurisdiction over appeals by contractors from findings of fact and decisions of contracting officers; patent policies and procedures; claims filed under the Federal Tort Claims Act; claims arising out of the survey, construction, operation, or maintenance of irrigation works; legal problems of a fiscal nature; legal questions arising under the statutes and regulations governing personnel matters; and the legal phases of matters considered in the  with respect to the programs and activities of the , the 

Bonneville Power Administration, and the Southwestern Power Administration. 

A3.07b. The Division of Public Lands has juris diction over the legal phases of matters considered 

1 Sponsored by Department of the Interior.

LO in the Office of the Secretary with respect to the programs and activities of the Bureau of Land Management, the , the Geological Survey, the Fish and Wildlife Service, and the Bureau of Mines; over appeals from the decisions of bureau officials in public-land cases; and over legal problems arising under the Connally "Hot Oil" Act.

A3.07c. The Indian Division has jurisdiction over the legal phases of matters considered in the Office of the Secretary with respect to the Administration of Indian Affairs in the continental United States and the Territory of Alaska. 

A3.07d. The Legislative Division coordinates and supervises within the Department matters which pertain to proposed or pending legislation affecting the Department or any of its agencies. 

Such matters include the development of legislative programs; the preparation of proposed legislation for submission to the Congress; the preparation of reports on pending bills to Committees or Members of the Congress; the clearance of proposed bills and reports with the Bureau of the Budget; the representation of the Department at 

Congressional hearings on legislative matters; the preparation of recommendations for the approval or veto of enrolled bills; the preparation of daily digests of the Congressional Record; the maintenance and indexing of the general legislative files; and the provision of legislative information for use within the Department.

A3.07e. The Administrative Branch has charge of the business affairs of the Solicitor's Office, and maintains the docket and records of the office. 

A3.08. Program Staff.-The Program Staff, under a Director, assists the Secretary in discharging his responsibility for formulating, recommending, and executing policies and programs within the jurisdiction of the Department. It is authorized to examine all policies and programs of the Department with the objective of ascertaining that (a) they are integrated and internally consistent; (b) they constitute a full utilization of the Department's powers for carrying out the responsibilities of the Department; (c) they are appropriately related to the programs and policies of other agencies of Government; and (d) they are in proper context with the current and prospective needs of the national economy.

A3.09. Division of Power. The Division of 

Power assists the Secretary to discharge his responsibilities with respect to the several power agencies of the Department, to the end that power is efficiently marketed to the best interests of the

Government. The Division aids in the formulation and coordination of power policies and practices of these power agencies; advises on power rates and contracts; reviews studies of the feasibility of new power projects and the allocation of their costs through proposed amortization plans; it assists the power agencies of the Department on economic and technical matters of unusual significance; and ascertains that the power policies of the Department are being carried out by the bureaus and offices of the Department concerned. Further, the Division negotiates power contracts for the disposal of power from projects in the Eastern United States developed by the Corps of Engineers, Department of the Army.

A3.10. Oil and Gas Division.-The Division is responsible for: (1) assisting the Secretary to (a) coordinate and unify oil and gas policy and administrative activities of the several Federal 

Departments and agencies, (b) serve as the channel of communication between the Federal Government and the petroleum industry, which is maintained chiefly through the National Petroleum Council and the Military Petroleum Advisory Board, (c) serve as the Government liaison agency with State oil and gas bodies, and (d) review and coordinate Federal policy with respect to technological developments in petroleum and synthetic hydrocarbon fuels; (2) keeping the Secretary informed about the adequacy and availability of supplies of petroleum and its products to meet current and future national needs, and about significant developments in the petroleum field, and making recommendations with respect thereto; (3) obtaining and analyzing information on oil and gas matters in which the Federal Government has a proper interest, and serving as the central clearing house for statistics, technical data, and other information; (4) coordinating all oil and gas policies and activities in the Department; (5) developing proposals looking to centralization of Federal oil and gas functions and activities.

The Connally "Hot Oil" Act, which prohibits interstate shipment of oil produced or transported in violation of State laws, is administered under supervision of the Division by the Federal Petro-

Organization Handbook leum Board with headquarters at Kilgore, Tex.

Suboffices are located at Midland and Houston, Tex., and Lafayette, La. 

A3.11. Office of Land Utilization.-The Di vision of Land Utilization assists the Secretary to fulfill his responsibilities for the land agencies of the Department. It coordinates and integrates land-use and land-management policies and programs, and assists in maintaining cooperative relationships with other Federal agencies and state and local agencies concerned with these policies and programs. The Office also serves as the staff for the Water Resources Sub-Committee of the Department's Program Committee. 

A3.11a. The Branch of Forest Management co ordinates all forest management and protection policies and programs on lands under control of the Department and advises the Secretary on forestry problems.

A3.11b. The Branch of Soil and Moisture Con servation and Range Management develops the broad soil and moisture conservation program of the Department, and assists the land agencies of the Department in formulating and conducting their individual programs. The Branch also assists the Secretary in developing and coordinating range management policies. 

A3.11c. The Branch of Lands coordinates the land activities of the Department, assists in developing basic land policies and programs, and advises the Secretary on land matters that require personal action by him.

A3.11d. The Branch of Budget and Finance performs the budget, accounting and administrative service functions for the Office. 

A3.12. Division of Territories and Island Possessions. The Division of Territories and Island 

Possessions coordinates the activities of the Department in the various territories of the United States. It has the responsibility for advising the 

President, the Congress, and the Secretary of the Interior on all aspects of the governments in these areas. It acts as liaison between the Territories and all branches of the Federal Government and the general public. It assists the territorial governments in working out plans and policies for a stable economy and a political status satisfactory to the local population. It aids in the drafting of legislation to be introduced in the territorial legislatures or in Congress. Also, the Division carries out the duties assigned by Congress to the Secretary for the care and maintenance of the Alaska insane, and it assists him in supervising the operations of the Alaska Railroad, the Alaska Road Commission, the Virgin Islands Company, and the 

Puerto Rico Reconstruction Administration, as described in sections A13.00 to A16.00.

A3.12a. The Director acts as Administrator of the Puerto Rico Reconstruction Administration; 

as Managing Director of the Virgin Islands Corpo ration, and as President of the Board of Directors of the Alaska Rural Rehabilitation Corporation. 

He also is responsible for liaison with Congress and the Executive Departments on all matters affecting the Territories and Island Possessions. 

The Assistant Director acts for the Director in his absence and in general supervisory work; represents the Division in Departmental, interdepartmental and Congressional conferences, and assists the Director in organizational and program planning. He has major responsibility for directing the programs related to Alaskan development and for general supervision over The Alaska 

Railroad and the Alaska Highway programs.

A3.12b. The Chief Counsel provides legal serv ice and counsel to the Director of the Division, the several branches of the Division, Governors of the Territories and Island Possessions, members of territorial and insular legislatures, and administrative officials of the Territories and Island Possessions.

A3.12c. The Administrative Branch plans, co ordinates, and conducts staff work on all phases of administrative management and performs office services for the Division and for the territorial operating agencies.

A3.12d. The Alaska Branch coordinates all matters within the scope of the Division affecting 

Alaska; recommends and follows through on programs which will improve the economic, social, and political conditions in the Territory; performs the function of interdepartmental coordination with respect to Alaskan affairs of interest to the Department of the Interior, the Territory of Alaska, and the general public; acts as Washington representative of the Governor's office, The 

Alaska Railroad, the Alaska Road Commission, and is responsible for the care and custody of the 

Alaskan insane.

A3.12e. The Caribbean Branch coordinates all matters of like nature within the scope of the Division affecting Puerto Rico and the Virgin 1s-

lands. It acts as Washington representative of the Governors' offices and supervises the work of the Washington representatives of the Virgin 

Islands Corporation. 

A3.12f. The Pacific Branch similarly coordi nates all matters within the scope of the Division affecting the Territory of Hawaii, as well as the 

Islands of Howland, Baker and Jarvis, and the

British-American Condominium of Canton and Enderbury. Under Presidential directive the 

Department also is responsible for planning and organizing civil administration for Guam, American Samoa, and the Trust Territory of the Pacific Islands to be set up when organic legislation is enacted. It keeps in contact with those persons in the general public concerned with the immediate and future problems of Hawaii and the Pacific 

Islands and acts as Washington representative of the Governor's office.

A3.13. Division of Geography. The Division of Geography performs the operational responsibilities of the Secretary of the Interior who, conjointly with the Board on Geographic Names, has the duty of standardizing geographic names in the United States and throughout the world for the mapping, intelligence, and other agencies of the Federal government concerned with geographic names. The Director of the Division is ex-officio Executive Secretary of the Board on 

Geographic Names. 

A3.13a. The Branch of Research makes such studies as are necessary for proper decisions on place names, their orthography, place-name policy, and related matters. 

A3.13b. The Branch of Records and Services promulgates place-name decisions and rulings, maintains records, furnishes information on geographic names to Federal agencies and the public, and performs administrative services for the Division and the Board. 

A3.20. Division of Budget and Administra tive Management.

The Division of Budget and 

Administrative Management exercises Depart mental control over the budget, finance, and administrative management activities of the bureaus and offices of the Department. In addition, it conducts investigations involving alleged irregularities.

A3.20a. The Branch of Budget conducts the budget program of the Department, consisting of formulating budget policy and procedure, preparing instructions for budget submissions, reviewing such submissions, preparing recommendations for action by the Secretary, and maintaining relationships with the Bureau of the Budget and Congressional appropriation committees involving budget estimates.

A3.20b. The Branch of Finance reviews and assists in developing systems for the control of appropriations and allotments, and develops financial reporting methods; it considers and makes recommendations on financial matters requiring Secretarial action, and analyzes and recommends improvements in the finance operations of the bureaus and offices. 

A3.20c. The Branch of Organization and Meth ods provides assistance to the Secretariat and the heads of bureaus and offices on organization problems; it reviews personnel actions in key positions for conformity with organization plans; it develops and manages a system of Secretarial and other administrative orders, and makes surveys of basic operating procedures throughout the Department. 

A3.20d. The Branch of Investigations conducts all investigations undertaken at the Departmental level involving alleged irregularities and misconduct of a criminal nature; and conducts special administrative investigations upon request from the Secretariat.

A3.21. Division of Information.-The Division of Information is responsible for a unified and effective information program and policy in the Department. The Director of Information has technical supervision over all information activities of the Department's bureaus and offices and arranges for the coordination, supervision and dissemination of useful information on the economic, research, service, construction, conservation, utilization, and other programs of the Department as they relate to public interest and the Nation's natural resources. The Division provides information service for newspapers, press associations, magazines, radio networks and stations, motion picture companies, and other media or individuals concerned with the Department's activities. The Division may initiate Department-wide information programs calling upon bureau and division heads for technical assistance. 

The Division supervises the editing and processing of public announcements and press releases through existing facilities, such as bureau and division information offices.

Organization Handbook 

Recently a Library of the Department of the Interior was established through consolidation of numerous bureau libraries. During the organization period the Library reports to the Director of Information.

A3.22. Division of Personnel Supervision and Management.-The Division of Personnel Super vision and Management is responsible for planning, organizing, directing, and supervising a comprehensive program of personnel administration. 

The staff of the Division counsels with the Secretariat on personnel management problems and gives advice to the operating agencies of the Department.

A3.22a. The Branch of Inspection conducts a personnel inspection program throughout the Department to obtain compliance with personnel laws, orders and standards and to assist in the improvement of methods of operation.

A3.22b. The Branch of Classification performs all staff functions with respect to position classification and wage rates. 

A3.22c. The Branch of Employment and Train ing develops broad programs for examination, recruitment, selection, and training in conjunction with the operating bureaus and offices. 

A3.22d. The Personnel Office conducts personnel operations for the .

A3.23. Division of Administrative Services.— The Division of Administrative Services provides office services for all units of the Department located in Washington. 

A3.23a. The Chief Clerk, who is head of the

Division, promulgates and enforces regulations of a general nature and controls operating services at the Department level including the assignment of space, expenditures from the contingent and printing and binding funds, communications service, photographic service, museum and dispensary; and controls the accounting, mail, files and motor vehicle services for the Office of the Secretary. 

A3.23b. The Branch of Property and Procure ment performs all purchasing and contracting, and manages property purchased from the Department's contingent account and other accounts when delegated; advises bureaus relative to property and procurement matters; requisitions, receives, stocks and ships printed material and issues stock items.

A3.23c. The Branch of Miscellaneous Services performs duplicating services for all bureaus and offices in Washington.

ESTABLISHMENT Creation. The Bureau of Indian Affairs was created in the War Department in 1824 and transferred to the Department of the Interior at the time of its establishment in 1849 (9 Stat. 395).

The purpose of the Bureau of Indian Affairs is to discharge the responsibility assumed by the United States by treaty or otherwise for the orderly incorporation of American Indian groups and individuals into our national life. Its main objectives are: the creation of conditions under which the Indians will advance their social and economic adjustment in the complex world in which they find themselves; the adaptation of native institutions and attitudes to best secure such adjustment; the organization of Indian tribes to enable them to manage their own affairs; the termi-

General description. The Bureau of Indian 

Affairs is composed of a headquarters organization in Washington, D. C., 11 area offices, and several score field units. The field units include 

Indian agencies, non-reservation schools and hospitals, and large irrigation projects, each of which is administered by a superintendent. Superintendents are responsible to an Area Director, who in turn is responsible to the Commissioner of Indian Affairs. There are some field units, so limited in function or so distant from any area headquarters, that they report directly to the 

Washington Office. 

HEADQUARTERS ORGANIZATION (1) Resources. 

(2) Community Services. 

The Division of Resources, responsible for conservation, development and utilization of re- 11

Organization Handbook sources, and the Division of Community Services, responsible for Health, Education, Welfare and

Tribal Relations, are each under the immediate direction of an Assistant Commissioner. The

Administrative management staff is under the immediate direction of the Executive Officer.

A4.01. Office of the Commissioner.-The Office of the Commissioner interprets and implements national policy on Indian affairs. It prescribes objectives and standards of performance, approves integrated programs designed to accomplish the Bureau's objectives, and exercises general direction and control over field operations. 

The unit consists of the Commissioner and his immediate staff assistants, the Chief Counsel, and the Information Officer.

A4.02. Chief Counsel.-The Chief Counsel is responsible for rendering legal advice and assistance to the Commissioner and his staff. 

A4.03. Information Officer.-The Information 

Officer is responsible for handling requests from the public and the Congress for information about Indians and about general activities of the Indian Service. He is responsible for preparing current news releases and for aiding the Commissioner in the execution of Indian Service programs by preparing timely explanations of programs and changes.

A4.04. Division of Resources.-The Division of Resources, under the direction of an Assistant Commissioner, is responsible for the general direction, coordination, and control of activities relating to the development, protection, and utilization of Indian resources. The work of the division is divided among a number of staff branches, each concerned with a major function of the Bureau. 

These branches are responsible for formulating and revising policies, procedures and standards and for developing plans, reviewing operations, and coordinating activities related to the function. described for each. 

These branches are responsible for formulating 

A4.04a. The Branch of Extension and 

Credit is responsible for activities providing agricultural and financial assistance for the Indians. 

The scope of work includes animal husbandry, horticulture, disease and pest control, home extension work, 4H Club work, and agricultural research and demonstrations, and loans. 

A4.04b. The Branch of Forest and Range 

Management is responsible for activities related to the management and protection of forest, range, and wildlife resources on Indian lands, and for the management and operation of Indian owned sawmills.

A4.04c. The Branch of Land is responsible for activities related to providing an adequate land base for Indian use, combining scattered holdings of individuals and groups into economic units in order to facilitate utilization and conservation, conserving and promoting the development of mineral resources, obtaining, through leasing, effective use of lands not used by Indian owners, regulating the alienation of Indian land, granting of rights of way, and protecting titles and claims. 

A4.04d. The Branch of Soil Conservation is responsible for activities related to the conduct of soil and moisture conservation operations. 

A4.04e. The Branch of Irrigation is responsible for activities of the Bureau involving the design, construction, operation, and maintenance of irrigation projects and related power systems. 

A4.04f. The Branch of Roads is responsible for activities related to the location, construction, and maintenance of roads and road systems on 

Indian lands.

A4.04g. The Branch of Economic Develop ment is responsible for the appraisal of the economic resources of the Indian tribes and for formulating long-range programs for the development and utilization of these resources. 

A4.05. Division of Administration.-The Di vision of Administration, under the direction of the Executive Officer, is responsible for the general direction, coordination and control of organization, budget, finance, property management, plant construction and maintenance, and personnel activities of the Bureau. The work of the division is divided among a number of staff branches, each concerned with a major function of the Bureau. These division branches are responsible for formulating and revising policies, procedures, and standards, and for developing plans, reviewing operations, and coordinating activities related to the function described for ecah. 

A4.05a. The Branch of Budget and Finance is responsible for activities relating to financial management within the Bureau including budget formulation and execution and fund accounting. 

A4.05b. The Branch of Property and Supply is responsible for activities relating to property acquisition, distribution, storage, and utilization

A4.05c. The Branch of Personnel is respon sible for activities relating to personnel management including classification, wage board administration, recruitment and placement and employee relations. 

A4.05d. The Branch of Buildings and Util ities is responsible for activities involving the design, construction, and maintenance of buildings and utilities.

A4.05e. The Branch of Management Plan ning evaluates the effectiveness and progress with which operating plans are executed and formulates recommendations for adjustments in policies, organization, and methods. It coordinates the development and installation of revised procedures and new organization plans. 

A4.06. Division of Community Services.-The 

Division of Community Services, under the direction of an Assistant Commissioner, is responsible for the general direction, coordination, and control of activities involving the health, welfare, placement, and education of Indians, law enforcement, and liaison with Indian tribal organizations. The work of the division is divided among a number of staff branches, each concerned with a major function of the Bureau. The branches are responsible for formulating and revising policies, procedures and standards, and for developing plans, reviewing operations, and coordinating activities related to the function described for each. 

A4.20. Area Offices. These offices embrace all activities of the Bureau of Indian Affairs within a defined area. Each area office is under the direction of an Area Director, who reports to the 

Commissioner of Indian Affairs. The Area Di rector is responsible for the operation of all activities of the Bureau's program. There are eleven 

Area Offices. 

FIELD ORGANIZATION 

Certain business operations and most technical supervision are provided at the Area level while program activities and tribal business operations which by their nature must be performed at the local level are conducted by the local field offices. 

Superintendents in charge of the field offices are directly responsible to the Area Director. There are four types of field offices as follows: (1) Agencies. An agency conducts all activities of the Bureau of Indian Affairs on one or more reservations, in contrast to the other three 

A4.06a. The Branch of Education is respon sible for activities providing educational opportunities to meet the needs of Indian children of school age in the continental United States and for Indian and native children of Alaska. 

A4.06b. The Branch of Health is responsible for activities providing medical services to Indians through numerous hospitals and clinics, and activities essential to lowering the incidence and severity of communicable diseases and reducing infant mortality.

A4.06c. The Branch of Welfare and Place ment is responsible for activities providing social services for needy Indians and for the development of programs designed to relocate Indians in communities where better economic and employment opportunities are available. 

A4.06e. The Branch of Tribal Relations is responsible for the correlation of plans of Indian communities and of the Bureau; for providing technical advice in perfecting Indian community organization, and for obtaining prompt and sympathetic review of tribal ordinances and resolutions requiring Departmental or 

Bureau approval. types of detached field jurisdictions whose activities are specialized. (2) Boarding schools. Such schools are established to provide certain types of educational facilities not otherwise available and educational facilities to students who need institutional care. 

Certain of these schools offer high school and vocational training courses. 

(3) Hospitals and sanatoriums. These hos pitals and sanatoriums provide institutional medical services to Indians in the area. Some serve as the center of service-wide health programs, particularly those specializing in tuberculosis. (4) Irrigation projects. These are major irrigation projects constructed primarily to serve Indian lands but they also supply water to non- 

Indian land owners. Incidental power systems supply electric services to the project and adjacent areas.

Organization Handbook 

A4.21. Detached field offices.-There are 10 detached field jurisdictions. Each of these, under the direction of a superintendent, is responsible for conducting the Bureau's program at locations too remote to be attached to an area office or for conducting a limited or specialized segment of the Bureau's program. Superintendents of such offices report to the Commissioner.

ESTABLISHMENT Creation. The President's Reorganization Plan No. III, effective July 16, 1946, created the 

Bureau of Land Management in the Department of the Interior by merging the General Land Of fice, established in 1812, and the Grazing Service, formed in 1934.

Purpose. The Bureau of Land Management is responsible for the management and custodianship of the public domain and its resources which include approximately three-quarters of a billion acres in the continental United States and Alaska, containing timber, mineral, grazing, and other resources. The Bureau plans and coordinates long-range programs for the proper conservation, Marion Clawson. 

Roscoe E. Bell. 

Jacob N. Wasserman.

Mrs. Norma R. Hazeltine. 

A5.01. Director.-The Director of the Bureau of Land Management develops long-range-programs designed for the proper management, development and conservation of the public domain. This includes recommendation for new and amended legislation. The Director also develops and implements policies and programs expressed in the various statutes and supervises the entire 

Bureau staff.

Lowell M. Puckett. use, and disposal of the public land based on the greatest public interest, bearing in mind all the various important factors, such as national security, social and economic factors, and other relevant matters.

General description. The Bureau organiza tion comprises a headquarters in Washington, D. C., 7 regions, 17 district land offices, 53 grazing district offices, 12 public survey offices, and nine forest offices. The Chief Executive of the Bureau is the Director. The line of administrative authority extends from the Director to the Regional 

Administrator to the head of the local office.

HEADQUARTERS ORGANIZATION A5.02. Office of the Chief Counsel.-The Of fice of the Chief Counsel reviews and advises on all legal matters affecting the Bureau, including legislation and regulations. 

A5.03. Information Officer. The Information 

Officer carries out the information program of the 

Bureau and advises the Director and other officials of the Bureau on matters pertaining to public information. 17

Organization Handbook A5.04. Division of Adjudication.-The Divi sion of Adjudication recommends policies and standards to be used in the adjudication of applications covering use and disposal of the land. 

It examines and acts upon some classes of applications and claims involving patents, leases, or other instruments relating to mineral resource development, land exchange, patenting of entries under the homestead, mining and other public land laws, withdrawals and restorations, or other forms of land use or disposal. Further, it has technical responsibility for other classes of applications which are acted on in district land offices. 

A5.04a. Branch of Land Use and Disposal.— 

Adjudicates applications for the use of the public land culminating in the issuance of patents, granting of fee title, issuance of leases, permits, licenses, rights of way, easements, etc., except those for minerals, grazing, and timber. The authority for adjudicating a large segment of these types of applications has now been delegated to the regional administrators. The Branch recommends the policies and standards to be adopted in adjudicating all these types of cases. This includes such matters as color of title and riparian claims, reclamation and irrigation matters, townsites, parks, State grants for educational, institutional or other purposes, railroad grants, timber and stone entries, quit-claim deeds, Indian lands, land exchanges, recreational sites, power sites, etc. This branch is also charged with the issuance of patents, amendment of patented entries, correspondence relating to patented entries and the furnishing of copies of official records. 

A5.04b. Branch of of Minerals.-Adjudicates matters arising under the Mining and the Mineral Leasing Laws including the adjustment of conflicts between mineral and non-mineral claims, the leasing or other disposal of minerals in certain acquired lands under the jurisdiction of the Department of Agriculture, the leasing for oil, gas and other minerals in certain acquired lands under the jurisdiction of the Bureaus and other agencies of the Department of the Interior, except Indian lands, and the leasing for oil and gas deposits in lands under the administration of other Departments, to prevent loss through drainage from wells on privately-owned lands, where such leasing has been authorized. The adjudication

The adjudication of non-competitive oil and gas leases as well as their assignments, collections and relinquishments has been delegated to the regional administrators. The 

Branch, however, recommends the standards and policies to be used in adjudicating all these types of cases.

A5.05. Division of Administration.-The Di vision of Administration handles matters relating to the budget, finance, personnel, management planning, and general administrative services. It coordinates all such activities of the Bureau. 

A5.05a. Branch of Personnel.-Develops and supervises a comprehensive Bureau-wide program of personnel administration.

A5.05b. Branch of Management Planning.— 

Develops, coordinates, and executes programs of organization, procedure, methods and practice studies throughout the entire Bureau. 

A5.05c. Branch of Administrative Services.- 

Handles matters of procurement, property, communications, and space.

A5.05d. Branch of Budget and Finance.- 

Develops and supervises all budget and finance operations of the Bureau and formulates and executes a system of budgetary administration including the apportionment of appropriated or allotted funds to operating divisions and approval from the fiscal viewpoint of their planning and scheduling with respect to programs.

A5.05e. Branch of Records.-Plans and coor dinates a long-range program covering all recordkeeping activities of the Bureau in Washington and the field. This includes maintenance of tract books.

A5.06. Division of Engineering.-The Division of Engineering develops policies and programs for, and has technical direction of, cadastral engineering surveys and resurveys on the public lands. Approves special technical instructions specifying survey procedures, and approves and accepts for the permanent record all types of cadastral surveys and resurveys. Has the custody of the official plats and field notes of all cadastral surveys executed under the direction of the Bureau in the United States and Alaska. Coordinates and standardizes cartographic and drafting work. 

Compiles and prepares for publication the official wall map of the United States and maps of the public land states. The Division designs and supervises construction of facilities for use in the field.

A5.07. Division of Forestry.-The Division of 

Forestry develops policies, programs, standards,

Bureau of Land Management 

19 and procedures with respect to management and conservation of timber resources and the protection of all surface resources from fire, trespass, insect pests, and diseases. sources.

A5.07a. Branch of Forest Management.- 

Plans and coordinates general programs for the proper management and disposal of forest re- These include the inventory and appraisal of forest and woodland resources, research studies in silvicultural practices and operational techniques, approves forest land exchanges and cooperative sustained-yield forest units, cooperates with private owners, States, and Federal and Territorial agencies as related to timber land and woodland management, and inspects forest management activities and organizations for adequacy and conformance.

A5.07b. Branch of Forest Protection.-De velops general programs for protection from fire, insects, and disease of all renewable resources. It reviews and recommends approval of fire contracts, protection programs, and plans for fire, disease, and insect control, and studies pertaining thereto. It determines the suitability and effectiveness of protection procedures, develops protection of unprotected areas; coordinates protection activities among regions and districts; prepares regulations, instructions, and policies for protection; and inspects protection areas and organizations and reviews actions taken for adequacy and conformance.

A5.08. Division of Grazing.-The Division of 

Grazing develops the policy and technically supervises the program with respect to grazing on the public lands both inside and outside of grazing districts. The Division promotes the economic use of the Federal range through proper range management.

A5.08a. Branch of Grazing Supervision and 

Surveys. Coordinates and supervises the work of regional organizations in conducting rangemanagement work to determine the allocation of grazing privileges, issuance of leases, licenses, and permits, cooperates with Federal, State, and wildlife associations in the preparation of game management plans, supervises and inspects all rangemanagement and range-improvement activities including reseeding, water development, eradication of poisonous plants, destructive rodents, and predatory animals, and erosion control:

A5.20. Regions.-The field organization con sists of seven regions, each one of which is under the direction of a regional administrator whose staff supervises and coordinates all Bureau activities within the area. The actual operating activities of the Bureau are for the most part conducted in the various local field offices. The regional 

A5.08b. Branch of Soil and Moisture Conser vation. Correlates and supervises the work of regional organizations in conducting the soil and moisture program which is designed to rehabilitate overgrazed and damaged range areas through reseeding, gully control, water spreading, and other measures, executes programs financed by contributed funds involving cooperation with State and county officials and advisory boards, and cooperates with the Office of Land Utilization in carrying out all soil and moisture projects. 

A5.09. Division of Land Planning.-The Divi sion of Land Planning develops programs for the most efficient classification of lands for agricultural, grazing, mineral, and other purposes. It supervises general research on the use of public lands including the technical supervision of field examination functions. The Division recommends policies and procedures for the utilization of all the public lands and their resources, including lands in Alaska.

A5.09a. Branch of Research.-Conducts gen eral economic research into past and present conditions of the public domain for use in administration and in the determination of future land policy; maintains public land statistics and analyzes and interprets results, and prepares reports. 

A5.09b. Branch of Land Classification.-For mulates broad land classification policy and programs, provides technical assistance on land classification matters, and coordinates the land classification work of the bureau field offices.

He recommends to the Director the proper use or disposition of land after a thorough consideration of all physical, economic and social factors in-

A5.21. Types of Local Field Offices.-There are four specialized types of field offices as follows: The various heads of these offices report to the regional administrator. 

1. Public survey offices. 

2. District land offices. 

3. District grazing offices. 

4. Forestry offices, including Oregon and 

California offices (Region No. 1 only).

A5.22. Public Survey Offices.-There are 

12 public survey offices in the United States and one in Alaska, each in charge of an office cadastral engineer. Public survey offices maintain plats and field notes of land surveys for reference by the general public. The office also furnishes copies of records, and arranges for mineral surveys.

A5.23. District Land Offices.-There are 17 

district land offices in the United States and three in Alaska, each in the charge of a manager. 

In states where no district land offices are located, all applications involving public lands are received by the Bureau of Land Management, Washington, D. C. Action on these matters is taken by the regional office (VI) in Washington which supervises the area involved. District land offices receive all applications and claims concerning land in the district and payment of required fees and 

A5.30. Requests for information concerning activities and functions of the Bureau should be addressed to the regional administrator in charge of the area involved or to the Director, Bureau of 

Land Management, Washington, D. C. 

Inquiries relating to the status of particular tracts of public land should be addressed to the appropriate district land office in the state in which the land lies. rentals. They are also the local source of information and records relating to the public lands. 

Action is taken locally on small tract leases; rightsof-way; approval of construction in advance of granting rights-of-way; noncompetitive oil and gas leases; applications for mineral spring leases; public sales of isolated or rough and mountainous tracts; special land use permits; homestead and desert land applications (subject to classification).

Other types are referred to the Division of Adjudication in the Headquarters Office for the necessary action. Patents, leases, and decisions are sent to the managers for delivery to applicants and claimants.

The various field offices will furnish information regarding their particular activities and refer inquiries and requests to the proper office. 

A5.24. District Grazing Offices.-There are 53 

district grazing offices, each in the charge of a district grazier. District graziers receive and act upon applications filed by stockmen for grazing permits. They initiate basic plans for the utilization and improvement of their districts and supervise the execution of these plans. District graziers initiate plans for fire protection, direct fire-control measures, and supervise all other activities involving the conservation of all surface resources in their area.

INFORMATIONAL SOURCES A5.25. Forestry Offices.-These include pri marily units established to facilitate the administration of the Oregon and California Revested

Lands but include also small units established elsewhere to handle timber work at local levels. Five district offices are maintained to manage these revested lands under a sustained-yield forestry program. The activities of these offices include land classification, forestry, grazing, fire suppression and prevention, timber sales, timberland exchanges and special land use permits.

Washington, D. C.
