ESTABLISHMENT 

Creation. The Bureau of Mines was established in the Department of the Interior by statute on May 16, 1910. The Bureau was transferred to the Department of Commerce in 1925 and was returned to Interior by Executive order, effective April 24, 1934, under statutory authority for reorganization of the Executive Branch.

Purpose.-The Bureau, a "bureau of mining, metallurgy, and mineral technology," promotes safety in the mineral industries, aids conservation of mineral resources, and conducts investigations on the mining, preparation, and utilization of minerals. By developing and introducing safe practices and improvements in mining and in the use of mineral substances, the Bureau seeks to promote the progress of the industry through scientific, A6.01. Director.-The Director of the Bureau of Mines formulates policies and exercises general direction and control of all programs.

A6.02. Office of Minerals Reports.-The Office of Minerals Reports directs informational activities, operates the library, and supervises the motion picture and publication programs of the Bureau. 

A6.03. Administrative Division.-The Admin istrative Division plans and coordinates the administrative management functions of budget, finance, property, reorganization, personnel, and office services, as well as all general business phases of field stations and offices and safe and healthful working conditions for employees. 

A6.03a. The Field and Property Branch plans, organizes, develops, and administers a Bureauwide program with respect to property, purchasing, contracts, supplies, and equipment and coordinates the general business phases of all field stations and offices. 

Paul M. Ambrose.

Carl E. Wood. 

Hewitt Wilson.

Ray G. Knickerbocker. 

Stuart R. Zimmerley. 

J. Bruce Clemmer.

Henry G. Iverson. 

A6.03b. The Personnel Branch plans, organizes, and directs a comprehensive program of personnel administration, advises on policies of management technical, and economic studies in laboratory, office, and field.

HEADQUARTERS ORGANIZATION General description.-The Bureau of Mines consists of a national headquarters in Washington, D. C., with a Director in charge and various activities outside of Washington. The national headquarters, known as the Office of the Director, is composed of six principal divisions and one information office. The six divisions deal with administration, health and safety, fuels and explosives, mining, metallurgy, and economics and statistics. The investigative and research work is conducted in the field (outside of Washington, D. C.) at special offices and installations designed to carry out this work. in its relation to personnel, conducts studies in these matters, and serves as a liaison with the Department on personnel management and general manpower problems.

A6.03c. The Budget and Finance Branch is re sponsible for the preparation of and assists in justifying the annual and special budget estimates; maintains appropriation controls and allotments controls; handles all fiscal audit and accounts work of the Bureau; prepares regulations and general instructions on these subjects; and interprets orders and regulations issued by the Interior Department, the Treasury Department, the Bureau of the Budget, and the General Accounting Office.

A6.04. Office of Chief Counsel.-The Office of 

Chief Counsel functions as the general legal counsel of the Bureau. All matters requiring legal consideration are referred to this office for review and advice.

A6.05. Economics and Statistics Division.- The Economics and Statistics Division plans, co ordinates, and directs the Bureau of Mines econo-

Bureau of Mines 25 mics and statistics program which is designed to provide Government, industry, and the public with factual information on minerals. The results of the work are published in numerous weekly, monthly, quarterly, annual, and special reports, including Minerals Yearbook-the Government's annual summary of progress in the Nation's mineral industries.

A6.05a. The Accident Analysis Branch com piles, analyzes, and publishes data on employment and the causes, frequency, and severity of accidents in the mineral industries; on the production of industrial explosives; and on the use of permissible and other explosives in the production of minerals. 

Work includes technical analysis of the causes of accidents, determination of accident-frequency and accident-severity rates for various types of operations, and the correlation of these rates with underground conditions, mining methods, types of machinery, and composition of the working force. 

The Branch also conducts safety contests that are sponsored by the Bureau of Mines and industry groups to stimulate the promotion of safety in. the mineral industries, makes detailed studies of accidents in coal mining as required by the Coal Mine Inspection Law, prepares annual data on accidents in metal and nonmetal mines and quarries, including processing plants, and compiles statistics on the production and use of explosives. 

A6.05b. The Coal Economics Branch publishes weekly, monthly, and annual data on the production of bituminous coal and lignite, employment, stocks, and consumption by industries; and makes fundamental studies of industry trends, competition in the soft-coal markets, trade practices, mechanization, and other economic and technologic factors. The Branch performs similar functions for anthracite, coke, coal chemicals, fuel briquets, packaged fuels, and peat; it also assembles technical and economic information for monthly publication in International Coal Trade and compiles annual data on the geographic distribution of anthracite consumption. It prepares for publication detailed data on bituminous coal and lignite, showing distribution by district of origin, method of transportation, market area, sizes of coal, and type of use, and, as required, as sists in the preparation of special studies of a broad economic nature. The professional staff of the Branch gives extensive consulting services to other agencies of Government on problems relating to national defense, international coal supply, domestic coal shortages, labor conditions, etc.

A6.05c. The Foreign Minerals Branch conducts a program with respect to the foreign aspects of mineral economics and statistics. It compiles and prepares for publication and for use of Bureau regional and commodity specialists and others, statistics on mineral resources, production, and foreign trade of all foreign countries and on United States imports and exports. The regional specialists of the branch prepare analytical reports on the resources and mineral industries of foreign countries. These are published annually in Minerals Yearbook and as special reports required in connection with national security, conservation, and international relations programs. In collaboration with the technical Divisions of the Bureau of Mines, the Branch promotes and administers technical research activities abroad that are conducted under programs authorized by law. 

A6.05d. The Metal Economics Branch conducts a program for the primary and secondary metal industries, including mining, milling, smelting, and refining operations; data on metal use and inventories also are collected from consumers and published at regular intervals. The Branch prepares significant information on mineral developments in foreign countries for monthly publication in Mineral Trade Notes. In addition to the established periodic reports the Branch's commodity specialists from time to time make special studies on reserves and resources, production problems, supply, demand, prices, impact of technologic changes, costs, international problems, etc. They also serve on various interagency committees, such as the Munitions Board industrial mobilization planning groups, and are frequently consulted on stock-pile procurement, legislative, and other Government matters. The Branch deals with programs relating to iron and steel and the ferroalloy minerals and the Nonferrous Metals Section with all other metals. Four field offices are maintained to facilitate the collection of statistics and the dissemination of information to the mining industry, and to prepare State reports. 

A6.05e. The Nonmetal Economics Branch con ducts a program for the nonmetallic mineral industries, which present a wide variety of technical and economic problems. Information on production, consumption, prices, uses, stocks, and trends in technology and demand are accumulated for

Organization Handbook virtually all the nonmetallics, particular attention. being given the availability, use pattern, and inventories of those nonmetallic minerals that are classified as strategic by the Munitions Board. 

The information gathered is analyzed and published annually in Minerals Yearbook as chapters, which show the place of each commodity in our mineral economy, the effect of technologic changes and other trends, our reserve position, etc. For a few commodities, monthly, quarterly, or semiannual market reports are issued. The Branch also makes analytical studies of special problems and publishes information circulars that are designed to facilitate the marketing of nonmetallic minerals. Information on developments in foreign countries is compiled and published monthly in Mineral Trade Notes. The Branch provides expert assistance to the military and other Government agencies directly and by service on interagency committees and assignments of specialists to work in occupied countries. 

A6.05f. The Petroleum Economics Branch con ducts a program for the oil and gas industries, including the production, refining, and marketing of these commodities and their products. It compiles weekly data on crude oil stocks and detailed monthly and annual statistics on production, refining, foreign trade, stocks, and demand of crude oil and oil products; and issues monthly and annual forecasts of demand for crude petroleum by States and for principal products. The Branch assembles detailed monthly and annual data on production, consumption, and stocks of natural gas, liquefied products, and carbon black and compiles and analyzes extensive information on foreign production, refining, trade, and prices of petroleum and its products. These data are interpreted and released in miscellaneous publications. A field office is maintained in Los Angeles to facilitate the compilation of west coast statistics and to give service to the industry in that area. 

As required, the Branch makes special studies of various technical and economic problems relating to oil and gas, and the specialists of the Branch are consulted frequently by military and other officials of the Government.

A6.06. Fuels and Explosives Division.-The Fuels and Explosives Division plans, coordinates, and directs research on the composition, handling, manufacture, and use of fuels and explosives and directs engineering services dealing with coal, petroleum, natural gas, synthetic liquid fuels, mine explosions, and military high explosives. The Division also directs the helium production program as well as the exploration program for deposits of coal.

A6.06a. The Explosives Branch conducts fun damental scientific research and tests on explosives and on development of new explosive mixtures. It conducts research and development work on detonators and primers and tests explosives and blasting devices to determine their permissibility for use in coal mines or suitability for use in other mining operations. The Branch also conducts research and tests on the explosibility and flammability of gases and vapors, the ignition of explosive mine atmospheres, and contamination of mine atmospheres by explosives. It determines the flammability and explosibility of coal, mineral, and other industrial dusts. The Branch investigates causes of disastrous explosions and, on the basis of its research work, devises methods for the prevention of gas and dust explosions. Full-scale tests are conducted in the experimental mine at 

Bruceton, Pa. Operating through six Sections located at Pittsburgh and Bruceton, Pa., the Branch cooperates with industry, associations, and scientific groups in the development of safer explosives and of safe procedures to minimize and eliminate disasters.

A6.06b. The Fuels Utilization Branch con ducts technologic and engineering studies and tests relating to the inspection, sampling, and utilization of fuels; provides assistance and consulting service to Federal agencies on problems relating to the purchase and efficient use of fuels, the storage of coal, and the purchase and operation of fuel-burning equipment, including research and service on boiler water treatment; and provides information along these lines to the general public. 

Three sections of the Branch are in the field at 

Pittsburgh, Pa., at Golden, Colo., and at College 

Park, Md.

A6.06c. The Petroleum and Natural Gas Branch conducts scientific and technologic research and performs tests on all phases of drilling, producing, transporting, refining, and utilization of natural petroleum hydrocarbons and their derivatives. 

This Branch also constructs, operates, and maintains plants that supply helium to Government agencies and other users; searches for, acquires rights in, develops, and conserves helium-bearing

Bureau of Mines 27 natural gas fields; and conducts research on the conservation, production, purification, and utilization of helium. Research on petroleum and natural gas problems is conducted at two experiment stations, three petroleum field offices, and one subfield office. A field headquarters office supervises a helium research laboratory and four helium production plants. The experiment stations, field offices, and field helium headquarters office report directly to the Branch while the sub-field office reports through a petroleum field office. Also the helium research laboratory and helium plants report through the field helium headquarters office to the Branch.

A6.06d. The Coal Branch conducts research and engineering investigations and tests on the chemical and physical properties of coal and on the mining, preparation, combustion, carbonization, and gasification of coal. This includes chemical, physical, and petrographic analyses to determine origin composition, classification, and preparation and use characteristics of coal; investigation of minable reserves of coal to aid in development of new coal sources, and the study of mining methods and costs to develop better and cheaper mining methods; washing and preparation of coals for general and special uses to develop most efficient preparation methods; research on the burning of coal in industrial and domestic furnaces to aid in the design of more efficient combustion equipment; research on coal carbonization including the study of the coke making properties and yields of byproducts from American coals; and study of the drying, storage, gasification, and utilization of coals, particularly the lower-rank western coals. 

This Branch operates through field sections at 

Pittsburgh and Schuylkill Haven, Pa., Tuscaloosa, Ala., Golden, Colo., Grand Forks, N. Dak., and Seattle, Wash.

A6.07. Office of Synthetic Liquid Fuels.-The Office of Synthetic Liquid Fuels is responsible for laboratory and pilot-plant research, process and engineering studies, and the construction and operation of demonstration plants to produce synthetic liquid fuels from coal, oil shale, and other substances, thereby establishing the necessary cost and engineering data for the development of a synthetic liquid fuels industry. 

A6.07a. The Oil Shale Research and Demon stration Plant Branch.-The plant branch, Rifle, Colo., conducts laboratory, pilot-plant, and dem- 846667-49- -3 onstration-plant investigation and research relating to the chemistry of oil shale and shale oil, the retorting of oil shale, and the refining of shale oil to develop methods and to furnish industry the necessary cost and engineering data for the development of oil-shale resources. 

A6.07b. The Oil Shale Mining Branch.-The branch at Rifle, Colo., conducts engineering and economic studies to develop efficient, low-cost methods of mining oil shale, including laboratory and experimental oil-shale mining studies and analyses of other mineral mining operations; mines oil shale for use in the oil-shale demonstration plant; and conducts any studies of mining needed in the synthetic liquid fuels program. 

A6.07c. The Research and Development Branch at Pittsburgh, Pa., conducts laboratory research and development work on the production of synthetic liquid fuels from coal and other substances, and with pilot plants makes process and engineering studies to ascertain lowest investment and operating costs and determines the best demonstration plan designs and conditions of operation. 

A6.07d. The Synthesis Gas Production Branch at Morgantown, W. Va., conducts laboratory and pilot-plant research and design studies relating to the production of synthetic liquid fuels from gases, including design, construction, and operation of equipment for the production and purification of carbon monoxide and hydrogen from coal, coke, anthracite, lignite, and natural gas for conversion to liquid fuels.

A6.07e. The Coal-to-Oil Demonstration Branch at Louisiana, Mo., plans and directs the over-all program pertaining to the production of synthetic liquid fuels and byproducts from coal; is responsible for and approves the designing, construction, operation, and maintenance of the minimum size coal hydrogenation, coal gasification, and gas synthesis plants which will allow the Government to furnish industry the necessary cost and engineering data for the development of these types of synthetic liquid fuel and byproducts industries. 

A6.08. Health and Safety Division.-The Health and Safety Division directs and coordi nates the activities of three branches constituting the Division. The personnel consists of engineers, coal-mine inspectors, chemists, physicists, safety instructors, clerks, etc., working in or out of 21 offices or laboratories in 18 States and Alaska and

Organization Handbook functioning to some extent in every State and in Alaska.

A6.08a. The Safety Branch promotes safety in the mineral industries by educational methods, advances knowledge of prevailing hazards and means of preventing injury and loss of life, and conducts engineering investigations on which its safety recommendations are based. The personnel of the branch presents courses in accident prevention, first aid to the injured, and mine-rescue methods and assists in rescue and recovery operations at mine disasters. It tests, for official approval, selfcontained respiratory-protective equipment and electrical equipment designed for safe use in gassy mines or explosive atmospheres. It investigates disasters in mines and plants of the mining and allied industries and makes recommendations to prevent similar occurrences. It conducts engineering investigations on flood control in the anthracite region of Pennsylvania.

A6.08b. The Coal Mine Inspection Branch ob tains and disseminates information on safety and health conditions in coal mines in the United States under the Coal Mine Inspections and Investiga tions Act of 1941 (Public Law 49). It inspects coal mines to determine the causes of accidents and occupational diseases incident to coal mining and makes recommendations for safeguarding the life and health of coal-mine workmen. It prepares and issues reports of inspections, investigations, and studies, and engages in educational activities directed toward the promotion of safe and healthful working conditions in the coal mines of the Nation.

A6.08c. The Health Branch conducts laboratory and field studies on sources, properties, determination, physiological effects, and control of atmospheric contaminants. The results of its studies are disseminated to assist the mineral and allied industries to improve the environment of workmen by controlling noxious gases, fumes, mists, vapors, dusts, abnormal temperatures, and humidity. It analyzes samples of gas and particulate matter and makes other chemical and scientific determinations in connection with inspection and investigations of coal and noncoal mines and other activities of the Health and Safety Division. 

It tests, for official approval, all respiratory protective devices except self-contained breathing apparatus, which are tested by the Safety Branch. 

A6.09. Mining Division.-The Mining Division investigates and develops mineral deposits, surveys the mining industry and resources of important districts, studies the technology of modern mining and milling, carries on physical research on basic engineering problems, and conducts experimental mining operations to advance the art. 

Operations are controlled through 10 geographically distinct branch offices completely covering the United States and the Territory of Alaska, and three branch offices having special functions Nation-wide in character. Each branch office reports directly to the office of the Division Chief. 

The 10 geographic branch offices, each including 2 to 13 States, plan and conduct the investigation and development of mineral deposits, make mining industry and district surveys, study current technology, and make experiments with mining methods and equipment. Each of the five large branch offices has one field office, in charge of a supervising engineer reporting to the branch chief, to provide close supervision of operations. headquarters facilities, and good industry contact. 

A6.09a. The Applied Physics Branch carries on physical research methods for the solution of broad mining engineering problems.

A6.09b. The Mount Weather Branch operates the Division's experimental mine on Mount

Weather, Va., and manages its minerals report file and investigations based thereon. This Branch also performs certain technical services for all other branches.

A6.09c. The Special Minerals Investigation 

Branch makes specialized investigations on a 

Nation-wide basis.

A6.10. Metallurgical Division.-The Metal lurgical Division directs and coordinates fundamental and applied research on the conservation. preparation, and utilization of metals and nonmetals, develops new metallurgical methods dealing with beneficiation processes, new techniques. and special equipment, evaluates promising projects through pilot plant testing, and analyzes and tests ore samples. Operations are controlled through branch field offices. 

The branch offices supervise and directs the work of the Metallurgical Division with respect to proj ects and problems applicable to their respective geographic locations or of more general interest: provide supporting services such as chemical. microscopic, and spectroscopic analyses, minera!

Bureau of Mines 29 dressing, and metallurgical testing. In addition, studies are made on the theory and improvement of equipment and processes for ore beneficiation.

Specialized studies are conducted as the need arises. 

A6.10a. The Albany, Oreg., Branch conducts research specifically on physical metallurgy of metals and alloys, improvement in techniques for producing alloys, thermal and electrolytic reduction of nonferrous metals, hydrometallurgical treatment of ores, high temperature processes for steel making and production of ferro-alloys, extraction of high purity rare metals from their ores and pilot-plant production of high purity zirconium metal. The Albany Branch also directs and supervises the Spokane, Wash., Field Office, the Nonmetallic Section at Seattle, Wash., and the Redding, Calif., Ferro-Alloy Pilot Plant. 

A6.10b. The Boulder City, Nev., Branch performs research mainly on electrolytic manganese and manganese dioxide, electrolytic zinc, pilotplant production of electrolytic chromium, and pilot-plant production of high-purity titanium, and evaluates promising laboratory processes through pilot-plant testing. The Boulder City

Branch also directs and supervises the San Francisco, Calif., Field Office.

A6.10c. The College Park, Md., Branch con ducts research primarily on processes to make pigment-grade titanium oxide from low grade ores, corrosion of titanium and zirconium metal, physical metallurgy of metallic titanium and titanium. alloys, and utilization of nonferrous secondary metals, such as aluminum and magnesium, from foundry waste, drosses, and scrap. 

A6.10d. The Minneapolis, Minn., Branch investigates processes principally for the commercial concentration and metallization of low-grade domestic iron formations, such as occur on the Mesabi range. The Minneapolis Branch also directs and supervises the Rapid City, S. Dak., Field Office. 

A6.10e. The Norris, Tenn., Branch conducts research on electric firing of ceramics, preparation of refractories, utilization of clays and shales for lightweight aggregate, preparation and properties of synthetic mica and synthetic cordierite, and processes to produce industrial diamond substitutes. The Norris Branch also supervises the 

Raleigh, N. C., Experiment Station, which conducts pyrometallurgical and hydrometallurgical research on metals and nonmetallics. 

A6.10f. The Rolla, Mo., Branch conducts research on mineral-wool fibers, physical metallurgy of magnesium and colbalt alloys, use of electrolytic manganese as an alloy agent in ferrous and nonferrous alloys, studies of arc welding, treatment of Southeastern Missouri cobalt-nickel resources, matte smelting titanium oxide ores for conversion to titanium chloride, and metallurgical processing in the zinc-lead industry. The 

Rolla Branch also directs and supervises the 

Bauxite, Ark., Pilot Plant. 

A6.10g. The Salt Lake City, Utah, Branch conducts research on metallurgy of titaniferous magnetite, thallium, chromium, and vanadium, extraction of vanadium from phosphate ores, pyrometallurgy of Idaho cobalt ores to produce high grade cobalt concentrate, and special researches in ore dressing. The Salt Lake City Branch also directs and supervises the Denver, Colo., Field Office and the Laramie, Wyo., Sponge Iron Pilot Plant. 

A6.10h. The Tucson, Ariz., Branch conducts research mainly on methods of expanding perlite and evaluating of products for industrial purposes, also development of a commercially feasible method for utilization of oxidized and mixed oxide-sulfide and electrolytic reduction of nonferrous metals, hydrometallurgical treatment of ores, high temperature processes for steel making and production of ferro-alloys, extraction of high purity rare metals from their ores and pilot-plant production of high-purity zirconium metal. The Albany Branch also directs and supervises the Spokane, Wash., Field Office, the Nonmetallic Section at Seattle, Wash., and the Redding, Calif., Ferro-Alloy Pilot Plant.

A6.101. The Tuscaloosa, Ala., Branch conducts research on the valuation and utilization of clays, on the determination of physical properties of clays; on beneficiation of heavy beach sands, graphite, and other nonmetallic minerals; and on the beneficiation of southern iron ores.

A6.11. Metallurgical Experiment Stations.- 

The functions of the Metallurgical Experiment Stations have been described above under the respective branch offices. In addition, experiment stations located at Reno, Nev., and Berkeley, Calif., report directly to the office of the Chief of the Metallurgical Division, and each has specialized

Organization Handbook lines of endeavor. The Metallurgy of Steel Sec tion, Pittsburgh, Pa.

, also reports directly to the 

Chief, Metallurgical Division. 

A6.11a. The Reno, Nev., Experiment Station conducts research to recover and conserve precious metals such as gold, silver, and platinum from ore bodies, solutions, and fumes.

A6.11b. The Berkeley, Calif., Experiment Sta tion conducts research on modifying, purifying, synthesizing, and proving the identity of special high purity substances used there for precise calo- 

A6.20. Functions.-Each of the field activities is in the charge of an engineer or specialist who is responsible for the conduct of the technical work assigned to him and who reports directly to his division chief in Washington, D. C. There are seven basic types of offices in the field. Some types are grouped by region, some by district, and others operate by "function" and do not observe geographical boundaries. 

A6.20a. Branch.-The branch offices supervise and direct the work of their respective divisions having such offices. 

FIELD ORGANIZATION 

A6.20b. Field office.-Usually small installations, field offices perform specialized technical work on limited subjects. rimetric measurements, accmulation of data on specific heat, heat capacity, and entropy of metallurgical substances, and heat of formation and reaction data.

A6.11c. The Metallurgy of Steel Section, Pitts burgh, Pa., conducts fundamental investigations of reactions occurring in slags and metals in steel making at elevated temperatures, the effect of reducing agents in production of sponge iron, and the physical chemistry of open hearth steel production.

Requests for loan copies of motion picture films in the Bureau's library should be sent to the Supervising Engineer, Graphic Services Section, Bureau of Mines Experiment Station, 4800 Forbes Street, Pittsburgh 13, Pa.

Coal Mine Inspection reports are available for inspection at the appropriate district and subdistrict offices in the vicinity of the mine inspected. 

A6.20c. Plant.-Pilot, production, and demonstration plants produce specific mineral products such as helium and synthetic liquid fuel. 

A6.20d. Station.-Established by law as "experiment stations" of the Bureau, this type of office is larger than a field office and has several specialized lines of endeavor. 

A6.20e. Laboratory.-Equivalent to experiment stations.

A6.20f. District. The district offices carry out work of the Health and Safety Divisions. 

A6.20g. Subdistrict. The subdistrict offices are under the Health and Safety district offices and their location is determined on an economic basis as to work load and proximity to certain coal fields.

PLACES TO OBTAIN INFORMATION

Information concerning the policies and programs of the Bureau of Mines, including such publications as reports and bulletins dealing with activities and investigations that are issued for free distribution, can be secured by addressing the Director, Bureau of Mines, Department of the Interior, Washington 25, D. C.

Requests for copies of publications that are for sale should be directed to the Superintendent of 

Documents, Government Printing Office, Wash ington 25, D. C. 

AND MAKE REQUESTS 

Limited supplies of publications on specific subjects are sometimes available at the office or source of origin in the field.

Washington, D. C.

ESTABLISHMENT Creation.-The  (for merly the United States Reclamation Service) was established in 1902 to administer the Federal Reclamation Laws in the arid and semiarid States of the West.

Purpose. The Bureau of Reclamation, through the utilization of land and water resources, promotes the development of agricultural and industrial economies. The Bureau constructs and operates multiple-purpose projects providing water for irrigation, hydroelectric energy, control 

HEADQUARTERS A7.01. Commissioner.-The Commissioner of 

Reclamation directs and supervises the irrigation and power programs intended to develop arid areas. in the West. To assist him in directing the development and planning of programs, setting effective standards of operations and in judging the effectiveness of operations, there are two Assistant

Commissioners and a technical staff of twelve

of floods, aids to navigation, water for domestic and industrial uses, and other purposes. 

General description.-The Bureau of Reclama tion is composed of a headquarters staff located in 

Washington, D. C., and in Denver, Colo., seven regional offices and a number of operating offices, including districts, projects, and field offices. The 

Commissioner of Reclamation is the chief executive of the Bureau, and has direct supervision over the headquarters staff and the regional offices. 

The head of the operating offices report to the respective regional directors.

ORGANIZATION 

branches and offices. Each branch and office coordinates its phase of the Bureau's program, establishes basic policies and standards to guide field organizations, and reviews the effectiveness of regional operations in meeting the Bureau programs.

A7.02. Office of the Chief Counsel.-The Office of the Chief Counsel reviews and advises on all 36

legal matters of the Bureau. Due to the interrelated nature and variety of individual problems involved, the Office of the Chief Counsel has no formalized organization units. 

A7.03. Office of Management Planning.-The 

Office of Management Planning develops policies, standards, and procedures for the efficient coordination, control, and administration of Bureau operations.

A7.03a. The Organization and Procedures Divi sion formulates and recommends over-all organization plans and changes to meet current needs and future developments. And, it develops and coordinates adminstrative practices and procedures throughout the Bureau.

A7.03b. The Administrative Release Division plans, coordinates and controls the preparation and issuance of the Reclamation Manual and similar issuances.

A7.03c. The Administrative Review Division advises the Commissioner, his staff branches and offices, and the office of the regional director on the effectiveness of organization, procedures and management policies and arrangements. It aids the staff branches and offices in the Commissioner's office and in the regional directors' offices in interpreting and applying Bureau administrative policies and operating requirements in solving current problems. 

A7.04. Engineering Assistant.-The Office of the Engineering Assistant is the engineering advisor to the Commissioner and also deals with special engineering problems.

A7.04a. The Design and Construction Section performs liaison work for the Branch of Design and Construction in Denver and the Commissioner's Washington office. It analyzes and advises the Commissioner's office on technical engineering data in reports and other material relating to the design and construction of reclamation projects. 

Bureau on various technical committees of interdepartmental and intradepartmental character. 

A7.04c. The Engineering, Drafting, and

Graphic Services Section provides drafting, graphic and map making services for the Commissioner's office.

A7.04b. The Special Engineering Assignments 

Section deals with special project problems, which cannot be appropriately assigned to any other portion of the Commissioner's office, requiring engineering analyses and reports. It assists representatives of foreign governments and others interested in the reclamation activities to obtain technical information of the engineering phases of the Bureau's operations. And, it represents the

A7.05. Office of Information.-The Office of 

Information is responsible for the informational activities of the Bureau.

A7.06. Office of the Comptroller.-The Office of the Comptroller serves as financial advisor to the Commissioner, reviews all Bureau financial matters and procedures and directs the Bureau's auditing. The audit activity, which for administrative purposes is headquartered in Denver, embraces systematic financial audits of all Reclamation projects and other levels of financial activity. The audit also extends to periodic examinations of the books and records of water users, administrative bodies. The Comptroller's staff, including the auditors, is available for special assistance in matters of contract negotiation and settlement, land reclassification, and other special determinations.

A7.07. Office of Personnel.-The Office of Per sonnel develops policies, standards and procedures to guide the personnel activities of the Bureau, including recruiting, classification, training, labor relations, collective bargaining, and safety. The 

Labor Relations Officers who are concerned with the labor relations activities of the Bureau are located in the field.

A7.07a. The Safety Administration Division guides all safety and health activities throughout the Bureau, including contractors. It inspects operations at all levels to determine the effectiveness of the health and safety activities.

A7.07b. The Classification Division guides the position classification activities throughout the Bureau, which includes developing allocation standards and standard job descriptions; conducting organizational studies for classification purposes; and inspecting operating organizations in the administration of the position classification activities.

A7.07c. The Wage Administration Division guides the wage administration and labor relations activities of the Bureau, which includes developing wage rate criteria; inspecting operating organizations in the administration of the wage administration activities.

Organization Handbook A7.07d. The Employment and Training Divi sion guides the effective utilization of Bureau personnel, which includes recruitment, selection, placement, reassignment, promotion, transfer, employee training and reduction in force. It inspects operating organizations in the administration of these activities.

A7.07e. The Employee Relations Division guides the employee relations activities throughout the Bureau, which includes employee grievances, counseling, suggestions, rewards for superior accomplishment, health, employee services, efficiency rating and disciplinary matters. 

A7.07f. The Personnel Operations Division per forms personnel management functions for the 

Commissioner's office in Washington; reviews and processes field actions not within the appointing authority of field officials; processes all applications for employment in the Washington office; and performs such necessary functions for the accomplishment of personnel activities for the Commissioner's staff.

A7.07g. The Personnel Field Division (Den ver) supervises and staffs the central board of United States Civil Service Examiners for the Bureau and coordinates the activities of regional and local boards. It coordinates the execution, in the field services, of the Bureau personnel utilization activities; performs personnel management functions for the brances and offices of the Commissioner located in Denver. 

A7.08. Office of Supply.-The Office of Supply develops policies, standards, and procedures governing procurement, property utilization and management, and office services activities throughout the Bureau.

A7.08a. The Procurement Division guides the procurement of movable property, including supplies, materials, and equipment and services, other than personal, and exercises technical supervision over the performance of procurement activities throughout the Bureau.

A7.08b. The Property Management Division guides the utilization of all movable property of the Bureau, including planning, lay-out and operation of warehouses and storage yards; the planning and operation of shops for the fabrication, reconditioning, repair and maintenance of movable property; the transfer and disposition of property surplus to current and anticipated requirements for construction, operation, maintenance, and administration of fixed property and the maintenance of proper records of acquisition, accountability and disposition of movable property.

A7.08c. The Office Services Division guides the office services operations of the Bureau, including correspondence and records, selection and utilization of office equipment and supplies, library, duplicating, photographic, stenographic, and messenger services, space, communications, travel, and related office services; performs office services for the Commissioner's office in Washington and exercises technical supervision over the performance of office services activities throughout the Bureau. 

A7.08d. The Publications Division assists and advises with respect to the scheduling of manuscripts for publication, format and methods of reproduction for publications, and provides technical supervision over publication activities throughout the Bureau.

A7.08e. The Supply Services Division (Den ver), in addition to rendering technical guidance to all field offices on matters relating to procurement, property management and office management, provides general services for all offices of the Commissioner located in Denver.

A7.09. Office of Programs and Finance.-The 

Office of Programs and Finance develops policies, standards, and procedures to control activities relating to programs, budget, account, finance services, and the coordination of construction progress reports and engineering cost estimates throughout the Bureau. The primary function of the Commissioner's Office of Programs and Finance is the administrative coordination and integration of program recommendations and decisions, rather than the making of such recommendations and decisions. The regional offices remain responsible for supervising the initiation and development of proposed programs by district or project offices, and for controlling and summarizing project-level details relating to both programs and performance; and the Commissioner's branches and other offices remain responsible for the technical adequacy of proposed programs, and for the analysis of the technical aspects of performance. 

A7.09a. The Programs Division guides the development and scheduling of long-term and annual work programs; reviews work schedules; schedules the submission of budget estimates by the regional offices, consolidating them for presenta-

tion to reviewing authorities; the preparation, submission, and distribution of financial and physical progress reports, analyzing and interpreting them for administrative use.

A7.09b. The Finance Division guides the ac counting operations of the Bureau, maintaining controls on appropriations and allotments; issues allotment advice in accordance with aproved programs; reviews financial statements and reconciliations of Bureau accounts with the General Accounting Office; notifies the Treasury Department of cash requirements and maintains cash control accounts; and certifies consolidated financial statements and increment statements for overall Bureau operations. Provides all financial services essential to the operation of the Washington office, including certification of payrolls, travel vouchers, vouchers for purchase, and maintenance of leave and retirement records and accounts. 

A7.09c. The Denver Finance Division provides finance services for all branches and offices of the Commissioner's office in Denver, including accounting, cost keeping, allotment control, payroll services, and voucher preaudit and scheduling; reviews and consolidates budget estimates and justifications for the Commissioner's branches and offices located in Denver; maintains financial and accounting controls on the obligations and expenditures of the Denver branches and offices under approved allotments, suballotments and apportionments; acts as financial advisor to the Denver branches and offices on all fiscal and accounting matters. 

A7.10. Branch of Project Planning.-The 

Branch of Project Planning develops policies, standards, and procedures governing the determination, evaluation, and reporting upon investigations, of the engineering and economic feasibility of river basin and project development. 

A7.10a. The Engineering and Surveys Division guides the engineering phases of investigations, covering such items as surveys, mapping, geology, cost estimates, cost allocations, capacities of reservoirs, spillways, canals, drains and other features water use and allocation, consumptive use of water, drainage requirements, power production capabilities, artificial precipitation, and ground water. 

It assures suitable engineering plans for the development, control and utilization of water, land and power resources, and associated multiple uses, and that adequate coverage and appropriate emphasis is given to the various multiple purposes. 

It assists in the preparation and review of legislation affecting project feasibility, development of investigations, and requirements for design provisions for multiple purposes. And, in collaboration with other agencies, it assists in the preparation of regulations for the operation of flood control storage capacities in Bureau of Reclamation reservoirs.

A7.10b. The Economics and Resources Division guides the economics and resources phases of investigations covering such items as land classification, areas to be irrigated, land use, land development, quantity of water, irrigation water requirements, drainage requirements, repayment, benefits, comparison of benefits to costs, cost allocations, and effects of reclamation development on the local areas, and on the conservation and development of national resources. 

A7.10c. The Program Coordination Division supplies specialized functional direction on the programs of Project Planning, and coordinates such work in the field and in Washington. It develops programs of Project Planning among the seven regions; coordinates reports on Project 

Planning within the Bureau during and after their preparation by the regional directors, and clears various aspects of reports with Operation and 

Maintenance, Legal, Information, Finance, Design and Construction, and other appropriate units. 

It is responsible for seeing that such reports are coordinated within the Department, among the states and local interests, and among other Federal agencies. And, it assists the Commissioner in the negotiations and processing necessary for their formal transmittal to the Congress. 

A7.10d. The Hydrology Division (Denver) 

supplies specialized functional direction on all hydrological matters. It conducts research and special and routine studies in hydrology and sedimentation; provides services and specialists in hydrology for regions and other branches and divisions; conducts studies of availability of water for irrigation, domestic and industrial supplies, power and other purposes, and estimates future stream flow at potential storage and diversion sites. It reviews design data for adequacy of reservoir capacities, spillways, outlets and power plans in relation to water to be controlled; prepares systems of stream flow forecasting; collaborates on hydrometerological investigations; and

Organization Handbook prepares and furnishes technical assistance in water litigation.

A7.11. Branch of Design and Construction (Denver).—The Branch of Design and Construc tion develops policies, standards, and procedures governing design and construction activities throughout the Bureau. It prepares designs, estimates, and specifications, performs centralized laboratory and testing services, and administers construction contracts for certain projects because of the peculiarities and technical difficulties involved in their design and construction. 

A7.11a. The Administrative Engineering Divi sion (Denver) acts as the liaison group between the Branch of Design and Construction and the other offices of the Commissioner in Denver; formulates the design and construction portions of the Bureau program; maintains over-all control and coordination of production in accordance with the established program and related commitments and restrictions; reviews and issues specifications; provides technical editorial and staff information services; furnishes technical library facilities and assembles technical data from library sources; and provides tracing and engineering filing services. 

A7.11b. The Chief Designing Engineer (Den ver) supervises the execution of the design responsibilities of the Branch of Design and Construction. He recommends policies concerning design and delegations of design work to regional and other field offices; reviews design activities performed in field offices; reviews field design data; develops basic design assumptions; prepares quantitative estimates of construction materials and equipment; and prepares designs and drafts of specifications. He expedites the issuance of economic designs and specifications to meet program requirements and other commitments; and executes a program of continuing inspection of existing structures and facilities. 

A7.11c. The Chief Construction Engineer 

(Denver) supervises the execution of construction responsibilities of the Branch of Design and Construction. He reviews construction activities of the field offices; provides advice and assistance to construction engineers; assists in the delivery of tractors; represents the Chief Engineer in conferences with contractor's representatives and construction engineers; assists in the delivery of plans, specifications and government-furnished materials or equipment to meet construction requirements; prepares and adjusts cost estimates; analyzes and recommends extra work orders, orders for changes, and findings of fact on construction and long-form supply contracts submitted in draft form by field offices or design sections; supervises specialized grouting operations; maintains liaison with the Department of Labor to obtain acceptable wage rates to be included in construction contracts for which the Chief Engineer is contracting officer; reviews and determines the adequacy of safety measures incorporated in specifications and during construction; assembles and maintains records of construction progress; analyzes contractor's programs for feasibility; and, performs special assignments relating to construction and the administration of construction contracts.

A7.11d. The Research and Geology Division 

(Denver) supervises the execution of research, geological and materials testing responsibilities of the Branch of Design and Construction. It supervises field and laboratory investigations relating to design and construction, materials testing, study of scale models and other laboratory techniques to determine the most advantageous design, construction sites and selection and utilization of materials or equipment; and, performs research in canal linings, weed control, sedimentation and other facilities as provided by the Bureau's program.

A7.12. Branch of Power Utilization.-The 

Branch of Power Utilization develops policies, standards and procedures governing the planning and coordination of the development of hydroelectric power resources; operates and maintains power facilities; and disposes of electric power produced on Bureau projects and on projects constructed by other agencies for which the Bureau has been delegated power marketing responsibilities.

A7.12a. The Resources and Development Divi sion conducts broad studies on power needs to meet regional development programs, and on possibilities for power development: (1) to provide basic data to guide the development of regional and interregional power resources; (2) to facilitate the planning and execution of Bureau and regional power programs; and (3) for use by the Branch of Project Planning in connection with the power phases of project plans. It studies and makes recommendations on the integration of power facili-

ties and development programs between regions, and on the relation of irrigation water requirements with power load requirements. It reviews recommendations on the proportion of project costs that should be allocated to power and, in cooperation with other branches, formulates overall plans and standards on the financing and repayment of project costs from power revenues. 

It collaborates with Branches of Project Planning and Design and Construction in determining capacities and design features of project power facilities, and on electrical design and construction problems. It supplies functional direction on power resources and development activities throughout the Bureau.

A7.12b. The Marketing and Sales Division plans and schedules power marketing surveys to be conducted in the regions for use in formulating Bureau-wide programs; establishes procedures and related policies to control and direct Bureau power negotiations; develops standard provisions for inclusion in power agreements negotiated by regional offices to see that they protect Government interests, conform with established Bureau, Departmental and National policies, and to ascertain that they are sound with respect to the ability of the Bureau to meet these provisions. It collaborates with regional directors and with interested. offices in complex power contract negotiations, and on major power sales and market development problems and in the review of rate schedules to assure production of required revenue within legislative and policy requirements. Supplies specialized functional advice and information on power marketing and sales activities throughout the Bureau.

A7.12c. The Power Operation and Maintenance 

Division guides the operation and maintenance of Bureau power facilities including generating plants, pumping plants, substations, transmission lines, communication and dispatching facilities, to insure maximum development of power and the most beneficial utilization of water supply. 

It coordinates regional power output to provide required interchanges of power between regions; reviews proposed power contracts to determine operating requirements; formulates, develops and promulgates reporting procedures on plant performance and for analyses of plant operations. 

In consultation with the Branch of Design and Construction, it approves schedules for interregional major maintenance programs and formulates instructions regarding coordinated operations for such purposes. It studies and approves programs submitted for the modification of existing power system installations to meet changes in operating requirements. It collaborates with the 

Resources and Development Division to incor porate improvements developed through operating experiences in power development plans. It reviews accomplishments and supplies specialized functional direction on power system operation and maintenance throughout the Bureau. 

A7.12d. The Power Field Division (Denver) 

furnishes special technical assistance to the regions on major power transmission and operation and maintenance problems, equipment acceptance tests, and the operating arrangements and requirements of power lines, sub-stations, and power generating equipment. It develops minimum standards and instructions for the operation and maintenance of all installed Bureau power plants, including apparatus protection and plant personnel safety. It makes periodic inspections of power facilities, reviews designs and specifications of proposed power facilities, and collaborates with the Branch of Design and Construction on power operation and maintenance problems.

A7.13. Branch of Operation and Maintenance. The Branch of Operation and Mainte nance develops policies, standards, and procedures governing the operation, maintenance, and development of Bureau irrigation projects, including land use, settlement, prevention of land speculation, use of water, and repayment by water users of allocated portions of the construction costs. 

A7.13a. The Land Use and Settlement Division guides the land use and settlement activities on project lands throughout the Bureau and other lands served or affected to further a balanced agricultural development, to insure the maximum sustained productivity and use of irrigable lands, and to bring about their full settlement in a manner that will furnish suitable livelihood on familysize farms and in stable rural communities. Land utilization and settlement responsibilities extend to and include soil classification and reclassification, agricultural and farm management practices and related activities in cooperation with State and Federal agricultural agencies, provision of settlement opportunities, settler selection, assistance to settlers in becoming established, advice on

Organization Handbook credit and community facilities, soil and moisture conservation, public land predevelopment programs, demonstration farms, and other cooperative activities with Federal and local agencies.

A7.13b. The Irrigation Operations Division is concerned with irrigation and related reservoir water supply and drainage operations, including: schedules for the storage, regulation and distribution of water to obtain its full beneficial and economical use; the layout of systems to secure maximum results; and the maintenance, rehabilitation and replacement of existing systems and other matters connected with the storage, regulation and delivery of irrigation water. It maintains cooperative relations with other agencies concerned with: flood control, drainage, river regulation and navigation, water allocations, and releases of water for irrigation, electric power production, recreation, fish and wildlife and other purposes. 

A7.13c. The Allocation and Repayment Divi sion is concerned with repayment allocations and repayment contracts, and initiates action on special problems with respect to all activities throughout the Bureau concerned with: (1) allocations of irrigation, municipal water, and other costs as the basis for repayment of the investment; studies of the costs of irrigation works that are properly reimbursable; (2) analyses of the ability of water users to repay construction costs; (3) schedules of charges for use in repayment contracts which assure continued project operation, prevent land monopoly and speculation, and promote full settlement, sound development and beneficial use of project land and water resources; (4) standards for rates for sale or rental of water; and (5) analyses of methods of financing the construction, rehabilitation and replacement of irrigation and related water systems, (6) formulation, negotiation, and clearance for execution of new and amendatory repayment contracts.

A7.13d. The Economics and Statistics Division conducts economic studies and statistical analyses on all phases of Reclamation project developments, including the assembly and analyses of crop returns, results of project operation, economic conditions of settlers, agricultural marketing, and comparisons of regional development and economic conditions and trends with State and national conditions. It executes and coordinates programs on a Bureau-wide basis for economic studies, research and statistics (1) on regional and project operation and maintenance; (2) on local, state and national relationships of Reclamation developments to other irrigated agriculture, and to all agricultural production; (3) on the need for settlement opportunities on irrigated land and other general agricultural and economic factors for their influence on timing construction programs; (4) on irrigation practices, agriculture and other economic results; and (5) on existing and future irrigation developments in other countries that have an influence on Reclamation projects and practices in the United States, and (6) on project construction costs in relation to repayment and economic benefits. Much of the economic analysis is cooperative with State and 

Federal agencies.

A7.20. Offices of the Regional Directors.- 

There are seven regional offices, each under the direction of a regional director responsible directly to the Commissioner of Reclamation.

Within predetermined, broad Bureau policies and long-range programs, the regional director supervises the execution of the Bureau program in the region. Regional offices are organized in similar 

A7.13e. The Special Assignments Division fur nishes water users and others concerned with reclamation activities with current information pertaining to land and water resources development, including land settlement, land development, land classification, irrigation of crops, repayment, and weed control problems, by direct correspondence, and editing and publishing the Reclamation Era with the assistance of the Reclamation Era Advisory Board. Other special assignments include limited activities in property available to project settlers.

The branches and offices in the regional office have the same functional responsibilities and operate under the technical guidance and subject to the technical review of the corresponding branches and offices in the Office of the Commissioner. Regional responsibility is confined to operations within a major geographic subdivision; the regional di-

A7.21. Operating Offices.-Operating offices of the Bureau include any organizational entity below a regional office that is separately administered including district, area office, project office, area planning office, field office, and field station. The operating offices are responsible for most of the actual and basic work of the Bureau in carrying out assigned plans and functions essential to project development. Each operating office executes its assigned plans and functions under the technical guidance and review of the regional office and Commissioner's Office. Since the basic objectives and conditions under which these offices are established differ widely, no uniform continuing office organization structure exists. 

A7.21a. District office is an operating office directly under a regional office, provided to perform on a consolidated basis all Bureau activities within a geographical subdivision of a region. 

A7.21b. Area office is an operating office, directly

A7.30. Inquiries and requests in general. 

A7.30a. Inquiries relating to the Bureau's operating projects or to projects under construction or investigation may be directed to the Commissioner, Bureau of Reclamation, Department of the Interior, Washington 25, D. C., or to the Regional

Director in the area where the project is located. 

The address of these Regional Directors and the areas in which they supervise Bureau activities will be found in the Roster.

A7.30b. Inquiries concerning notices of public land openings on Bureau projects, settlement opportunities, sale of power from hydroelectric plants operated by the Bureau, requests for pamphlets and other material issued by the Bureau for free distribution may likewise be directed to the Commissioner or Regional Directors. under a regional office, provided to perform all Bureau activities in a defined area where the Bureau's program is largely limited to investigations. An area office has the essential characteristics of a district except there are no extensive construction or operating activities in the area.

A7.21c. Project office is an operating office, directly under a regional office, provided to construct, develop, or operate all or parts of one or several projects. 

A7.30c. Requests for copies of non-technical publications of the Bureau which are for sale should be directed to the Superintendent of Documents, Government Printing Office, Washington, D. C., or to the Commissioner.

A7.30d. Requests for technical publications on 

A7.21d. Area planning office is an operating office, directly under a regional office, provided primarily to conduct basin and project investigations within a defined area. 

A7.21e. Field office is an operating office under a district, project office, area planning office, or another field office.

PLACES TO OBTAIN INFORMATION 

A7.21f. Field station is an operating office consisting of an established place of work or headquarters for one or several employees engaged in a limited phase of a field activity. Field stations may be directly under a regional office or another operating office.

A7.30e. Requests for the loan of Bureau of

A7.31. Location of headquarters organiza tion. The Office of the Commissioner of Reclama tion is located in Washington, D. C., except the 

Branch of Design and Construction, which is located in Denver, Colorado.

ESTABLISHMENT Creation. The Fish and Wildlife Service was established in 1940 by Reorganization Plan III 

(54 Stat. 1232; 5 U. S. C. 133t), by consolidation of the Bureau of Fisheries and the Bureau of Biological Survey. These bureaus had been transferred to the Department of the Interior in 1939 from the Department of Commerce and the Department of Agriculture, respectively, by Re organization Plan II (53 Stat. 1433; 5 U. S. C. 

133t). The Bureau of Fisheries was initially established as an independent office known as the 

Commissioner of Fish and Fisheries in 1871 and retained its independent status until 1903 when it became a bureau upon the establishment of the 

Department of Commerce and Labor. In 1913, when separate Departments of Commerce and

Labor were formed, it continued as a bureau in the 

Department of Commerce. The Bureau of Bio-

Logan J. Bennett. logical Survey may be said to have originated by order of the Secretary of Agriculture in 1885. It was recognized as of bureau caliber in 1905 and retained bureau status in the Department of Agriculture until the reorganization of 1939. 

Purpose. The predecessor bureaus originally were established for research purposes, but in the course of years were also charged with carrying out the provisions of laws designed to increase the stocks of fish and wildlife, resist depletion, disseminate knowledge and extend the use of these living resources, and with the enforcement of protective laws relating to fish and game and the commercial fisheries of Alaska.

General description.-The Fish and Wildlife 

Service is composed of a headquarters office in Washington, D. C., six regional offices, and various types of individual field offices and stations. 47

Organization Handbook HEADQUARTERS ORGANIZATION A8.01. Office of the Director.-The Director of the Fish and Wildlife Service supervises and directs all the activities of the Service. He exercises immediate supervision over the divisions and branches of the headquarters staff as well as administrative supervision of the regional directors.

A8.02. Office of the Chief Counsel.-The Office of the Chief Counsel reviews and advises on all legal matters affecting the activities of the Service. 

A8.03. Division of Information.-The Divi sion of Information plans, organizes, and conducts information programs to acquaint the general public and special interest groups with the functions, objectives, and accomplishments of the Service.

A8.04. Division of Commercial Fisheries.- The Division of Commercial Fisheries plans and directs the Fish and Wildlife Service program of industrial research and services for the commercial fisheries.

A8.04a. The Branch of Commercial Fisheries conducts investigations to improve methods of catching, handling, preserving, storing, and transporting fishery products and byproducts. It also directs the collection and dissemination of fishery statistics; conducts economic research on costs, employment, prices, and marketing; and operates market news, educational and market development services. It conducts exploratory investigations and development projects concerning the fishery resources of the territories and island possessions of the United States in the tropical and subtropical Pacific Ocean; and conducts exploratory fishing activities off the coast of Alaska. 

A8.04b. The Branch of Alaska Fisheries plans and directs the regulation of the salmon and other important commercial fisheries of Alaska to yield maximum returns with a minimum of depletion and formulates and enforces protective measures to accomplish results. It manages the fur-seal herd and other marine mammals and provides for the maintenance and education of natives of the Pribilof Islands.

A8.05. Division of Management.-The Divi sion of Management is responsible for the conduct and coordination of all fish and wildlife management activities of the Fish and Wildlife Service.

A8.05a. The Branch of Federal Aid in Wildlife Restoration administers the Pittman-Robertson Federal Aid to Wildlife Restoration Act. It de termines whether projects submitted by States, Alaska, Hawaii, Puerto Rico, and the Virgin

Islands dealing with the acquisition and development of lands, research into problems of wildlife management, and maintenance of completed projects are approvable under the act. The Branch determines the proper shares of annual appropriations due program participants, in accordance with formulas provided by the Act, and reviews and approves audits and reimbursement claims. submitted for work performed on these cooperative wildlife restoration projects. 

A8.05b. The Branch of Game-fish and Hatch eries plans and directs the activities involved in the propagation and distribution of fifty different species of food and game fishes, lobsters, and mussels; the Lower Columbia River Fisheries Development Program; and the maintenance and operation of fish screens on Federal irrigation projects and works in cooperation with other agencies in formulating fish management programs for waters on areas under Federal Control. 

A8.05c. The Branch of Game Management plans and directs the administration and enforcement of Federal Statutes for the protection and conservation of wild birds, mammals and black bass and other game fish, and the Alaska Game 

Law, the Sockeye Salmon Fishery Act, and the

Act to regulate the landings, delivery, cure and sale of sponges. It is responsible for the issuance of permits to take, possess, buy, sell and transport migratory birds for scientific and propagating purposes; and also has charge of operations for the abatement of agricultural crop damage caused by unusual concentrations of wildlife. 

AS.05d. The Branch of Lands plans, coordi nates, and reviews the work of its field offices in maintaining boundaries, clearance of rights-ofway permits, appraisals, negotiations, surveys, and acquisition of lands. The Branch maintains records relative to land custodianship, prepares land status maps, and performs general drafting work for the Service.

A8.05e. The Branch of Predator and Rodent 

Control plans and directs cooperative predatory animal and rodent control operations throughout

Fish and Wildlife Service 

49 the United States and Alaska to protect livestock, poultry and game from destruction by predators and to prevent destruction of growing agricultural crops and range forage, horticulture, stored food and feed supplies by injurious rodents. It conducts field investigations and demonstrations in protecting game, livestock, and other domestic resources by the suppression of rabies and other animal-borne diseases transmitted by wild animals and cooperates with States, Counties, Agricultural and Livestock Associations in control operations. 

A8.05f. The Branch of Wildlife Refuges plans and executes a balanced wildlife program for the establishment of refuges for migratory waterfowl, big game, and other forms of wildlife throughout the United States and its possessions; conducts and coordinates investigations to determine methods of habitat improvement for wildlife; determines waterfowl and big game food resources; and gathers data for developing water resources on the refuges. It reviews general construction plans for refuges, supervises construction of water control and other impoundment structures, and is responsible for the direction of land use and the administration of the economic resources of refuge lands.

A8.06. Division of Administration.-The Di vision of Administration conducts all phases of business management for the Service. 

A8.06a. The Branch of Budget and Adminis trative Management supervises the budgetary program, coordinates plans and procedures governing administrative operations, and performs miscellaneous office services.

A8.06b. The Branch of Finance and Procure ment conducts the finance, procurement and property accountability services at the departmental level, and supervises the conduct of these operations by the field service. 

A8.06c. The Branch of Personnel plans, coordi nates, and supervises the program of personnel administration for the Fish and Wildlife Service.

It also performs centralized services. 

A8.07. Division of Research.-The Division of 

Research conducts technical research in the management and development of fish and wildlife resources.

A8.07a. The Branch of Fishery Biology plans, directs, and supervises Federal fishery research activities to discover the nature, extent and causes of variations in abundance of the important food fishes and other aquatic animals; for discovering depletion and recommending measures for conserving, developing, and managing the fishery resources, including improvements in fish-cultural practices, combating fish diseases and stocking and managing fish supplies in the interior waters; investigating the extent and nature of water pollution and developing control measures; improving the cultivation of aquatic animals, including shellfish, and recommending conservation measures. 

It designs fishways and other protective devices. 

It operates the Nation's only marine fishery research vessels, Albatross III and Black Douglas.

A8.07b. The Branch of Wildlife Research plans, directs, and supervises the wildlife research activities of the Service, including studies of life histories, habits and distribution of birds, mammals and other wildlife except fishes. It conducts studies on wild fur animals, waterfowl, upland game birds and big game species. It conducts investigations of damage caused by wildlife to agriculture and forestry, and devises methods for controlling injurious species. Studies of the Nation's wildlife resources, methods of conservation, restoration, and management are among its major activities. The Branch investigates the effects of insecticides and other control chemicals on wildlife populations.

A8.08. Office of Foreign Activities.-The Of fice of Foreign Activities advises the Office of the Director and other organizational units of the Service on foreign policy and international negotiations involving the fishery and wildlife resources; and represents the Director in the conduct of liaison and advisory services to the Department of State and other Federal agencies involving the formulation of programs and policy, and the conduct of international negotiations involving fisheries and wildlife. The Office administers the provisions of the International Convention for the Regulations of Whaling concerned with licensing, research and enforcement. The Office conducts technical missions in foreign countries at their request, and administers a rehabilitation and development program for the Philippine fisheries. 

In-service training programs for foreign students are administered by the Office in accordance with awards made by the Director upon recommendation of the Department of State, other organizational units of the Service cooperating in the actual training.

Organization Handbook A8.09. Office of River Basin Studies.-The 

Office of River Basin Studies investigates the probable effects on fish and wildlife of dams and other water control structures planned by the , the United States Corps of Engineers and other Federal Agencies, as well as those licensed by the Federal Power Commission. The 

Office makes data resulting from such studies available to interested agencies and submits appropriate recommendations for modification of construction and operational plans in order to prevent damage to or enhance fish and wildlife resources in the river basins.

A8.10. Office of Pacific Oceanic Fishery Investigations.-The Office of Pacific Oceanic Fish ery Investigations plans and conducts the fishery research and development program in the waters 

FIELD ORGANIZATION 

A8.20. Functions. In general, the headquarters of the Fish and Wildlife Service in Washington is concerned with the formulation of policies and programs while the actual conduct of program operations of the Service is performed by various types of field offices and stations. 

A8.21. Regions.-There are six regional offices, each in the charge of a regional director. Each regional director has general responsibility for the execution of approved programs by field organizations under his authority. Each regional director also formulates and recommends to the 

Director of the Fish and Wildlife Service pro posals for more effective administration and performs public relations functions within his region concerning the Fish and Wildlife Service.

The Regional Offices of the Fish and Wildlife 

Service have jurisdiction over activities in the areas listed below: 

(a) Region 1.-All Pacific Coast States, and in addition, Ne vada, Idaho, and Montana-Portland, Oreg. (b) Region 2.-The States of Wyoming, Utah, Colorado, Arizona, New Mexico, Kansas, Oklahoma, and Texas-Albuquerque, New Mexico. 

(c) Region 3.-The States of North and South Dakota, Ne braska, Minnesota, Wisconsin, Michigan, Missouri, Iowa, Illinois, and Indiana-Minneapolis, Minn. (d) Region 4.-The States of Arkansas, Louisiana, Mississippi, Alabama, Georgia, Florida, North and South Carolina, Tennessee, Kentucky, and Virginia-Atlanta, Ga. of the Territories and Island Possessions of the United States in the tropical and subtropical waters of the Pacific Ocean and intervening areas. 

The objective is to develop and sustain such fishery resources at maximum levels of production and to provide for the best possible utilization thereof. 

The program is exclusively of a research and fact finding nature and will be effectuated by continuing studies on oceanography, the biology of indigenous species of fish, shellfish and other aquatic animals, and by investigations of the technology and economics of the resources. The Office represents the United States Government on the Indo- Pacific Fishery Council which was established by interested governments for the purpose of coordinating fishery research and development in high-seas areas of mutual interest. (e) Region 5.-The New England States, and in addition, New 

York, Pennsylvania, New Jersey, Delaware, Maryland, West Virginia and Ohio-Boston, Mass. 

(f) Region 6.—Alaska-Juneau. 

The following field offices, stations, and areas within a region are responsible to the regional director:
